- Persisting a message encodes it once instead of twice. The session record, its error text and its file bytes are unchanged.
- A prompt's compaction check reads the session's messages instead of copying them: RPC turn allocations 3,472 -> 3,032.
- Appending a message counts it from its decoded entry instead of parsing its record again.
- Anthropic, Google, Mistral and OpenAI Responses requests encode each large earlier tool result once, as OpenAI-compatible requests do, so a long conversation's per-request CPU no longer grows with the size of its history.
- A long conversation's requests no longer read each earlier large tool result again to validate, hash and clean it; per-prompt CPU in a 60-prompt session drops about 12%.
- Whether a built-in or models.json provider has configured auth (model selection, session restore, extension `setModel`, virtual-model routing, the prompt auth check) now comes from the last published availability pass, as Pi's `hasConfiguredAuth` reads `snapshot.configuredProviders`; the prompt check then falls back to a live check, as Pi's does. A credential another process writes or removes is seen at the next pass or credential operation instead of immediately (model-runtime.ts:544-546).
- Each HTTP response body reader takes its 64 KB read buffer from the shared pool at its first read and keeps it until the body ends, fails or closes, instead of taking and returning one on every read, so a garbage collection in the middle of a stream no longer makes the next read allocate a fresh buffer.
- `chord/delta` is the only implementation of Pi's `@earendil-works/chord/delta` operations, as Pi exports them from one subpath. It now also holds `Apply`, `DiffRevisions`, `AssertSafePath`, `ReservedSegments`, `TypeError` and `DiffCandidate`, which were internal. An invalid operation is now a `*delta.TypeError` that carries Pi's message without a prefix, and `errors.Is(err, delta.ErrInvalidOp)` still holds for it. `Overlap` takes Pi's optional probe and candidate limits.
- Chord and Durable JSON objects keep Pi's key order. A decoded or built object is an ordered `*delta.JsonObject` (`chord/delta`), whose iteration follows JavaScript own-key order: array-index keys first in numeric order, then the other keys in insertion order. Before, objects were Go `map[string]any`, so diffs, snapshots and wire output listed keys in an order Pi does not produce. This is a breaking Go API change. `durable.JsonObject` is now `*delta.JsonObject`. `delta.Track` takes a `*delta.JsonObject`. Chord state values, document values and decoded operations hold `*delta.JsonObject` where they held maps. Through `encoding/json`, a `delta.Ops` batch and a `durable.DocumentContent` decode the objects their operations carry in order; a plain `[]delta.Op` field holds Go maps. Build objects with `delta.NewJsonObject` or `delta.JsonObjectOf`, decode JSON text with `delta.DecodeJson`, and read members with `Value`, `Get`, `Has`, `Keys` and `All`. `chord.CopyJSON` and `chord.CopyJSONObject` still accept a Go map and copy it in own-key order, with non-index keys ascending, because a Go map has no insertion order.
- Chord service-provider updates are written with Pi's member order (`{type, instance?, member, sequence, ops}` and `{type, snapshot|instance}`) instead of alphabetical order.
- `harness.ConversationView.Docs` is a read-only `harness.ViewDocs` instead of `map[string]durable.JsonObject`. It keeps Pi's key order: mounted documents in mount order (`pi.agent`, `pi.live`, `pi.inbox`, `pi.provider`, `pi.usage`), then documents set later in the order they arrive. Read it with `Get`, `Has`, `Len`, `Keys` and `All`, and build one with `harness.ViewDocsOf`.
- Durable records read back from JSONL or SQLite storage, and values decoded by `durable.FromJsonValue`, keep the key order of their JSON text in every `JsonValue` member: entry data, task input, checkpoint and outcome result, and submission detail. These members now hold `*delta.JsonObject` where they held `map[string]any`.
- Go `chord` package: replicated state follows Pi's `replicatedState`. `NewReplicatedState` creates mutable state whose listener failures are always reported asynchronously (never returned from `Change` or `Replace`), subscriptions accept asynchronous listeners (`SubscribeAsync`) with Pi's 100-delivery window, `Subscribe` returns only the unsubscribe function, and an attached state without `OnError` reports failures like an uncaught exception. `IsJsonValue` and `JsonValue` join the root export.
- Codemode results and `Sandbox.Close` now return once the VM is asked to stop instead of waiting for it to exit, following Pi 3daa2028 ([#10748](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/issues/10748)). A VM that does not reach an interrupt check is still halted after the half-second grace period.
- The release binary is 344 KB smaller: the QuickJS module that code mode runs in is embedded gzip-compressed and inflated on first use. The module's bytes are unchanged.
- An extension process that handles `message_update` now receives a streamed message as the changes since the previous update instead of the whole partial message twice per delta, and each SDK (Node, Go, Rust, Python) hands the handler the same full event as before. A streamed write of a long file with such an extension loaded costs time linear in the file's size instead of quadratic.
- The Session's event worker identifies its goroutine once instead of once per streamed event (formatting a goroutine's stack costs time proportional to its depth), which lowers the CPU of a long streamed answer. Which goroutine may yield for a stream result is unchanged.
- A streamed Markdown answer re-renders only the list items and quotes whose text changed, not every item on each frame, with the same output (a 20,000-character bullet list streams 2.7 times faster).
- Measuring a line with table borders or frame edges (`│`, `─`, `┼` and the other Box Drawing characters) is faster, so streamed Markdown tables and bordered screens render with less CPU. The measured widths are unchanged: each Box Drawing character is one cell, as in Pi, and a combining mark, variation selector or joiner after one still takes the exact path.
- Syntax highlighting of fenced code blocks is faster while an answer streams, with the same colours. highlight.js's joined matcher now tries at each position only the rules that can start with the next character (a 20,000-character TypeScript block streams 2.4 times faster, Python 3 to 9 times), and a block in a language whose rules cannot read across lines (Python, Go, JSON, Bash, CSS, SQL and 150 others) resumes from where the previous frame stopped instead of highlighting the whole block again.
- The extension SDK getters for the configuration root can fail. The Go SDK's `Context.ConfigHome` returns `(string, error)`, the Python SDK's `config_home` raises `RuntimeError`, and the Rust SDK's `Context::config_home` returns `io::Result<String>`, each when the home directory is needed and unavailable. The Rust SDK no longer reads `GOPI_HOME`. Set `PIG_HOME` instead. The Node.js runtime exposes the same rule as `getConfigRoot` in its `pig-config` module.
- The generated JSON Schemas (`schemas/*.schema.json`) carry Pi 1.2.0's `$id` URLs, `https://pi.dev/schemas/<file>`, in place of the old raw.githubusercontent.com paths.
- Closing a `ctx.ui.custom` overlay now removes the topmost overlay, as Pi's `hideOverlay()` does, even when the component that closed is a lower one; focus goes to the overlay left on top. Before, PiG removed the closing component's own overlay.
- A Durable submission that only writes an application entry (`kind` and `data`, no `model`, `head` or `edits`) is appended at once and settles `done`, also while the conversation is busy, unless a queued reset is pending; it no longer waits in `pi.inbox` (Pi `submissions.ts` display-only admission).
- The Cloudflare endpoint placeholders `{CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID}` and `{CLOUDFLARE_GATEWAY_ID}` are now resolved by `ai.CloudflareStreams`, which wraps the Cloudflare providers' API streams at registration as Pi's `cloudflareStreams` does, and `ai.ResolveCloudflareModel` resolves a model's endpoint. `ai.ResolveCloudflareBaseURL` is removed. A provider is no longer recognized by its ID inside the OpenAI and Anthropic API implementations.
- `durable.DefineDoc` and `durable.DefineDocFamily` panic on a `DocumentSemantics` that Pi's types cannot express: a history or fork on a session or task document, a conversation document without a history or fork, or a latest document that forks `asOf`. `durable.NewLatestConversationSemantics` and `durable.NewRewindableConversationSemantics` build the two conversation members.
- The Run Local Models on NVIDIA RTX page now lists its prerequisites first, including a check that `llama-server --help` lists `--models-preset` (Pi b9ab2a62).
- The Go SDK page now says `coding.DefaultAgentDir()` returns the `PIG_CODING_AGENT_DIR` folder when it is set, as the `pig` command and Pi's `getAgentDir()` do. It keeps the older-build workaround (leave `AgentDir` empty).
- The facade Harness of the Durable core host recovers from a Wasm trap: it drops the trapped instance, opens a fresh one from SQLite and runs the pending work again. A transaction that was open fails with a `RecycledError` that says nothing was committed, an operation that may have applied and cannot apply twice fails with `RecycledUncertainError`, and a second trap on the same event fails the conversation with the trap's error. An instance over its high-water mark is dropped at the host's next quiet point. A Durable Object session keeps its own recovery.
- Durable (Pi 15e7bdc1): the transcript a compaction summarizes (`SerializeConversation`, and the core's summary request) no longer includes replayed thinking. Providers return summaries of the real reasoning, and replayed thinking is often not part of the live context, so including it could make the summarization request larger than the context it summarizes.
- A Durable submission that only writes an application entry (`kind` and `data`, no content) is appended at once and settles `done`, also while the conversation is busy, unless a queued reset is pending; it no longer waits in `pi.inbox` (Pi `submissions.ts` display-only admission).
- `durable/images/node.CreateNodePhotonImages` takes `*NodePhotonImagesOptions` (`Worker`, `IdleMs`), as Pi's `createNodePhotonImages(options)` does. By default one worker goroutine prepares images one at a time and exits after `IdleMs` (default 30000) without a request; `Worker: false` prepares on the caller's goroutine. Pass nil for the defaults.
- Durable (Pi 42a3497d): `ToolExecutionResult.Content` is now `Output`, next to the new `StructuredOutput` for programs that call a tool. The built-in `pi.tool` task is version 2 with tagged input and result (`kind: "model"` or `"nested"`); a version 1 task migrates to `kind: "model"`. `bash` and `powershell` answer a nonzero exit with an error result carrying an `exit_code` diagnostic and a structured output instead of throwing.
- A Piglet can't strip every built-in tool and `/quit` together. `pig piglet validate`, `pig piglet build` and `pig --piglet` refuse such a strip list, also when it comes together through `extends`, and name the entries: keep at least one tool or `/quit`. Stripping `/quit` alone (Ctrl+D and a double Ctrl+C still exit) or every tool alone (a chat-only Piglet) stays allowed.
- Extension state pushes are smaller. The host sends a runtime the registered-tool, command and system-prompt-option catalogs only when they changed, skips a push identical to the last one it sent that runtime, and caches the serialized tool catalog until a tool is registered. On a 26-extension stack a run's state traffic fell from 80 MB to 25 MB and a turn's wall time by about 10%. Based on a proposal by Volker Mauel (#205).
- Consecutive listeners of one extension on the shared event bus now run from one request, over one payload object, as Pi's `emit` calls them in one loop.
- With several extensions loaded, a turn does less host work. The active model's metadata, the session information and the slash-command list that the host replicates to extension processes are encoded once and again only when the model, the session or a registered command changes, rather than on every push. With ten extensions a turn allocates about 9% less (30,970 to 28,150 allocations, 4.8 to 4.2 MB). Extensions still read the live values, including after a model switch by the user.
- `Context.SetEditorComponent` in the Go SDK takes an `EditorFactory` instead of `any`. The old `any` form accepted only nil, so only an extension that passed another value breaks, and `pig extension upgrade` reports it with the fix.
- The host writes each frame to an extension with one socket write and without allocating, which lowers the cost of every extension call and notification.
- `pig -p` and `--mode json` runs collect garbage less often when `GOGC` is not set in the environment: the Go GC target is 400 until a collection finds more than 64 MiB live, then the default 100. A short print run then usually finishes without a collection. Measured on Pi-Bolt's headless scenario: about 15% less CPU on a 32-core Linux host and 15% fewer instructions on an Apple M-series Mac, for 2-4 MB more peak memory (up to about 14 MB on a 50-200 KB file write). Interactive and RPC sessions are unchanged, a `GOGC` you set is used as is, and output is unchanged.
- Go API: `mcp/oauth` URLs are `oauth.URL` (WHATWG, as Pi's `URL`) instead of `*url.URL`. `StartAuthorization`, `ResourceURLFromServerURL`, `DiscoveryURL.URL`, `OAuthChallenge.ResourceMetadataURL`, `DiscoveryOptions` and `OAuthFlowOptions.AuthorizationServerMetadataURL`, `OAuthClientProvider.RedirectToAuthorization`, `OnRedirect`, and `coding/mcpext`'s `McpOAuthSettings.AuthServerMetadataURL` and `McpSignInPrompt.ShowAuthorizationURL` take or return it. Build one with `oauth.ParseURL`; `Href()` gives the text `url.Parse` accepts.
- A codemode result or console line holding an unpaired surrogate (`return "\ud800"`, `console.log("\ud800")`) keeps it in the tool result, the json events and the session file as Pi does (the escape `\ud800`), instead of turning it into U+FFFD.
- The OpenAI Codex WebSocket reads the lowercase proxy variables before the uppercase ones (`https_proxy` before `HTTPS_PROXY`), as Pi's undici proxy does and as PiG's HTTP requests already did; it used the uppercase one first when both were set.
- Starting pig allocates less memory. `--version` no longer reads the Piglet cell manifest. A `--model` that names its model no longer builds the whole startup model list. The bundled model catalog shares its repeated thinking-level and header maps. Indexes and tables that only some runs need (catalog lookup by bare id and by provider, image models, the models.json schema, the Mermaid block parser, the system theme rules, the errno name tables, the LaTeX symbol tables, the legacy key sequences, the file-extension language table and the legacy keybinding-name migrations) are built on first use.
- `chord/host`: a received frame is checked against one declaration of every frame, as Pi's: a wire error's code must be a Chord error code, and a hold's contract token must be a contract description (scope `remote` or `local`, object schemas, method/state/opaque member descriptions). Anything else is a malformed frame and closes the link (Pi 90f17f59).
- `chord/host`: `Link.Send` returns `(int, error)`: the size of the encoded frame as sent (UTF-16 JSON length, or bytes of a binary message), 0 when the transport does not know it (IPC) (Pi db6497c3).
- `pig piglet build` now names the external Go modules the fused vet did not check. The vet reads only the members' own modules and their workspace modules, so when a fused member requires other modules, the build prints one `note:` after the vet passes, listing each as `path@version` at the version the build selects, sorted by module path, with its replacement when a `replace` directive supplies its source. A build with no such modules prints nothing. The Piglet Binaries guide says what the vet covers and why.
- `agent.NewAgent` returns `(*Agent, error)`. It fails with `ErrNoDefaultStreamFunction` when no `StreamFn`, `DefaultStreamFn` or configured default exists, as Pi's `Agent` constructor throws. A model's `Provider` no longer stands in for the stream function.
- The TUI uses Pi's names and input pipeline: `ShowOverlay`, `GetFocusedComponent`, `AddInputListener`, `RemoveInputListener`, `OnTerminalColorSchemeChange`, `SetTerminalColorSchemeNotifications`, `RunInputListeners`, `ConsumeTerminalColorSchemeReport` and an exported `CompositeTuiLine`. `Start` and `Stop` re-enable and disable terminal light/dark reports, so the external editor, suspend and renderer swap no longer write them by hand.
- `tui.NewBranchSummaryComponent(summary)` is `tui.NewBranchSummaryMessageComponent(message, markdownTheme, outputPad)`, Pi's `BranchSummaryMessageComponent` constructor. The expanded summary now renders with the given markdown theme (it ignored one before), and `outputPad` is the Box's horizontal padding.
- `env.SshArgumentsWith(target, strictHostKeys, knownHostsFile)` is Pi's positional `sshArguments`; `SshArguments(target, options...)` keeps its options form and applies Pi's defaults.
- Long streamed answers use far less CPU and memory. Text, thinking and tool-call arguments are no longer copied again for every token, tool-call arguments are parsed from the bytes a token adds instead of from the start, and a published stream event copies only what changed since the previous one. On a stream of 50,000 text deltas and 20 tool calls the builder dropped from 3.4 s and 7.7 GB allocated to 0.15 s and 49 MB; the output is unchanged.
- The extension host reuses buffers for incoming frames of up to 1 MiB instead of allocating one per frame.
- Bedrock extended-thinking signatures are no longer rebuilt on every delta; a long signature costs time linear in its size instead of quadratic.
- `PIG_PROFILE=flight` keeps the last 10 seconds of the execution trace in memory and writes it to `crash-output/<pid>.flight.trace` beside the crash output when a session ends without a user request. It is off by default.
- Startup copies less: the embedded docs digest is computed once and streamed instead of read into memory twice, the SDK hash buffer is shared, and two SDK regular expressions compile on first use. `PIG_PROFILE=metrics` reports the process's allocation totals, and a test gates the allocation of `pig --version` and of a first prompt.
- The first provider error of a session no longer waits about 40 ms while the retry pattern compiles; it takes about 3 ms.
- Startup no longer compiles the Markdown parser's patterns: the Markdown packages the Mermaid parsers use are vendored with lazy patterns (about 160 KB and 1,500 allocations less at start).
- Typing in the prompt editor allocates 18% fewer objects per keystroke (60 to 49) and 24% fewer bytes; the rendered rows are unchanged.
- A tool schema containing a cycle, or one nested thousands of levels deep, now marshals through the ordered walk with an exact cycle check instead of a depth cap; output and errors are unchanged.
- The faux provider's usage estimate reuses each session's prompt storage and the pooled JSON encoder, so a model call over a 3,500-turn history allocates about a quarter of what it did (a warm Durable-bench turn: 46.8 MB to 12.6 MB, 2-3 collections to 0-1); token counts are unchanged.
- A shell command streaming many chunks of output no longer copies its retained tail for every chunk: appending a chunk to a full 50 KB tail took 95 us and now takes under 3 us, 80 percent of which is the full-output file write.
- A streaming write tool call now highlights only the new lines of its content, and the first 50 lines again as a block, as Pi does, instead of the whole file on every argument update (a 1,500-function Go file streamed in 60 updates: 1.35 GB allocated before, 48 MB after).
- An edit call that changes many places in a large file normalizes the file once and builds the result in one pass: 50 edits in a 160 KB file allocated 9 MB and now allocate 1 MB.
- The token estimate of a context (compaction check, usage estimate) counts the UTF-16 length of tool declarations and tool-call arguments without building their JSON text a second and third time, and `internal/jsstring` rewrites the encoder's text in place in a pooled buffer. One more turn of an RPC session against the faux provider allocates about 315 KB (3,350 objects) instead of 367 KB (3,490), measured on the same gate before and after with `-pgo=off`. A new gate, `TestExtraTurnAllocationBudget`, runs the built binary in RPC mode for 2 and 12 turns and holds the per-turn difference to a budget that only ratchets down.
- The RGI emoji sequence set of `tui/widthx` (2,760 strings, 109 KB) is built when a multi-code-point segment first needs it, not at process start: the package initializers allocate 1.34 MB instead of 1.53 MB with this and the goldmark case-folding map below. `TestPiWidthDifferential` against Pi's own width code passes, and a build with the set emptied fails it.
- The case-folding map of the vendored goldmark (`util.DoFullUnicodeCaseFolding`, 82 KB) is built on first use, not in a package initializer; it matches upstream goldmark on 3,000 random strings.
- The allocation and startup gates build `pig` with `-pgo=off`, so an untracked `cmd/pig/default.pgo` in a developer's tree no longer changes what they measure.
- `ls` skips an entry whose name is not valid UTF-8, as Pi does (Node's `readdir` decodes the name with U+FFFD and the `stat` that follows misses the file), instead of listing the raw bytes; `find` prints one U+FFFD for a truncated multi-byte sequence in a name, not one per byte. Both are checked against Pi's tools on a real directory, and `grep` on non-UTF-8 paths and lines matched Pi already.
- Tool-card output is stripped of terminal escapes by copying runs of kept bytes instead of one byte at a time: a clean 50 KB output returns unchanged (0 allocations, was 1 × 57 KB; 168 → 33 µs), colored output 137 → 82 µs; checked against the byte loop on 200,000 random escape/control/UTF-8 strings.
- Path completion (`@` and direct paths) lists a file name that is not valid UTF-8 with U+FFFD for each ill-formed subpart, as Node's `readdirSync` hands it to Pi, instead of the raw bytes; a symlink with such a name stays a file because the stat on the decoded name fails, as in Pi.
- Every sort that mirrors `localeCompare` goes through one `jsstring.Collator`, which places U+FFFD after all other code points but U+FFFF as ICU does (the x/text tables put it between `-` and `1`, so `ls`, completion lists, model and resource lists ordered a name containing U+FFFD differently from Pi). Other differences between the x/text tables and Node's ICU (newer scripts and symbols) remain; `internal/chord` keeps its own x/text collator because it may not depend on files outside the package.
- `nodefs.ReadDir` hands out entry names decoded as UTF-8 like Node's `readdirSync`, and the skill and prompt-template loaders take an entry's type from the directory entry (stat only for a symlink) and read the decoded path, as Pi does: a skill or template file whose name is not valid UTF-8 is no longer loaded (Pi skips the skill directory and warns `ENOENT … open '<decoded path>'` for the file; an explicit prompt directory ends its load at the first such name because Pi's source lookup throws), and these open failures carry Node's `ENOENT: no such file or directory, open '…'` text.
- The directory collectors that mirror Pi's package-manager, extension-loader, skill and prompt-template discovery (`coding/packagecontent`, `coding/extension/source`, `coding/discover_extensions.go`, skill and prompt-template loaders) list entries as Node's `readdirSync` does: decoded names, type from the directory entry (`nodefs.EntryKind` stats only a symlink). A file named with invalid UTF-8 is listed under its decoded path, as Pi lists it, instead of its raw bytes (the extension, prompt and theme files of such a directory resolved to paths Pi never produces); a directory so named is entered at its decoded path and found missing.
- The current-directory session list (`SessionManager.list`, `/resume`) reads file names as Node's `readdir` decodes them, so a session file named with invalid UTF-8 is left out of the list as in Pi (PiG listed it under its raw-byte path).
- `jsstring.Collator` / `LocaleCompare` order a literal U+FFFF after U+FFFD as Node's ICU root collation does (they used to tie). U+FFFD becomes U+FFFF plus a tab and a literal U+FFFF becomes two U+FFFF before the x/text comparison, which keeps every ordinary character below both and U+FFFD below U+FFFF in any position. A Node differential over 20,000 random strings of noncharacters, unassigned code points, case, accents and CJK finds no difference; U+10FFFF and Tangut still differ from ICU because the x/text tables are older.
- Tool argument validation compiles the `^name$` pattern of each Object property once per cached schema instead of once per validated object (c5ba51c214 compiled it for every property of every array item: validating 1,000 edits cost 196,910 allocations, now 100,847, the same output).
- `ToFixed` formats with strconv when the digits+1 expansion shows the value is not a tie, and takes the exact big.Rat path only for a possible tie: an ordinary cost (0.123456, 4 digits) allocates once (9 B) instead of 45 times (816 B). The result is the same string; a differential against the exact path covers 120,000 values and ties.
- The Codex SSE request body is compressed with zstd level 3 as Pi's `compressRequestBodyZstd` does; PiG used to send the JSON in uncompressed (stored) zstd blocks, so a long session's request was several times larger on the wire (a 2 MB body: 2,031,770 bytes against Pi's 29,246; now 39,408 for that repeated-turn body and within 10% of Pi's size for varied text). One lazily built klauspost encoder with a 2 MiB window (the level 3 window) serves all requests; it retains 3.7 MB after its first use.
- The SSE line splitter remembers how much of a pending line it has searched and finds a line ending with one `IndexByte` for LF and one for CR instead of `IndexAny`: a 16 MB line delivered in 4 KB reads decodes in 46 ms instead of 24 s, and in 64 KB reads in 36 ms instead of 1.6 s. The lines (LF, CRLF, lone CR, a CR at the end of a read) are the same for every chunking.
- Go SDK: a provider object's credential is `*sdk.Credential` instead of `map[string]any`, in `APIKeyAuth.Login`, `OAuthAuth.Login`, `OAuthAuth.Refresh` and `OAuthAuth.ToAuth`, `APIKeyAuthInput.Credential`, `RefreshModelsContext.Credential`, and `Provider.FilterModels`/`FilterAllModels`. A credential keeps the order of its keys and of the keys of every object inside it, so `auth.json` stores a Go login's or refresh's credential in the order the provider gave it, as Pi and the Node, Python and Rust SDKs do. Build one with `sdk.NewCredential("type", "api_key", "key", key)`. Read it with `Get`, `String` and `Keys`. Refresh it with `Clone` and `Set`: an existing key keeps its place and a new key goes last, as `{ ...credential, key }` does. An extension written for `map[string]any` no longer builds, and `pig extension upgrade` names each changed field with this remedy.
- **Breaking (Go API):** a host-process Go extension's `extension.BeforeAgentStartEvent.SystemPrompt` is an `*extension.SystemPrompt` instead of a string. Its `Get()` returns `(string, error)`: the prompt the run's shared options render when read, as Pi's `event.systemPrompt` getter does, so a handler sees its own edit through `extension.BeforeAgentStartOptions(ctx)` at once; before, it read the prompt rendered before the handler ran. An invalid custom section makes `Get()` return the error, and a handler that does not read the prompt still runs; before, the handler was skipped with that error. Migration: `evt.SystemPrompt` becomes `evt.SystemPrompt.Get()`.
- `RemoteOverlayOptions` in the Go extension SDK is no longer comparable: it carries `OnHandle`, a function field, and a pointer to `OverlayOptions`. Compare the fields you need or use `reflect.DeepEqual`; do not compare it with `==` or use it as a map key. The `struct-not-comparable` upgrade rule covers it.
- Interactive mode's extension state pushes reuse the settings encoding made once per settings change instead of encoding the settings again before every tool, command and event dispatch; the pushed bytes are unchanged.
- The JSON Schema validator is a vendored fork, a package of the root module (`third_party/jsonschema`, notice in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`). It compiles the draft metaschemas when the first Compiler is created instead of at package initialisation. Process start no longer spends 1.7 MB in 22,362 allocations (about 2 ms) on them: the binary's package initialisers drop from 3.7 MB / 40,736 allocations to 2.0 MB / 18,566. Validation results are unchanged; the fork's own test suite passes. The changes from upstream v6.0.2 are in `third_party/jsonschema/FORK.md`.
- Comparing the auth.json revision no longer formats a string: a credential read that finds the file unchanged allocates 4 times instead of 9.
- The GitHub Copilot proxy-endpoint pattern compiles on first use, not at start.
- `durable/interop/bench.mjs` reports the pi-durable version that resolved, not a fixed `1.0.4`. It and `bench/durableperf` report the process CPU time of the open and of each measured turn (`openCpu`, `turnCpu`).
- Typing in the editor allocates less. A key typed inside a word at the end of a line allocates nothing. One printable key, its frame render and a restoring backspace went from 60 allocations and 15 KB to 6 allocations and 2.3 KB, and from about 29 µs to 15 µs. An unchanged editor frame went from 32 allocations to 1. The rendered output is unchanged.
- `agent.ConvertToLLM(messages)` is Pi's `convertToLlm`; the provider normalization (dropping errored assistant messages, filling orphaned tool calls) is `agent.NormalizeMessages(messages, model)`, which callers run before `agent.ConvertToLLM`.
- A `before_agent_start` handler's edit to `systemPromptOptions` (a section, the selected tools) is in the system prompt the next handler reads from `event.systemPrompt` and `ctx.getSystemPrompt()`; the prompt is rendered from the shared options each time, as Pi does.
- `imageprocessing.ResizedImage.Data` is the image's base64 text, as Pi's `ResizedImage.data`; it was the raw bytes.
- A system message that only user messages precede leads the model context, so a later tool change no longer invalidates the whole provider prompt cache.
- `ToolExecutionApi.Output` takes an optional skipped marker, like Pi's `output(chunk, skipped?)`; the separate `OutputSkipping` method is gone. `RegisterEnvConformance` takes an options struct for the shell and symlink support.
- `durabletest.StorageConformanceAssertions.Ok` takes `(value any, message ...string)` and checks JavaScript truthiness, as Pi's `ok(value: unknown, message?: string)` does.
- `extension.API.OnTurnEnd` handlers return a `TurnEndEventResult` (an alias of `BoundaryResult`), so an in-process `turn_end` handler can propose boundary entries and a continuation as Pi's `ExtensionHandler<TurnEndEvent, TurnEndEventResult>` does.
- `extension.ProjectTrustContext` (cwd, mode, hasUI, and the select, confirm, input and notify of the UI) and `extension.ProjectTrustHandler` are Pi's types. `inproc.EmitProjectTrust` passes the context to every `project_trust` handler, and `API.OnProjectTrust` takes a `ProjectTrustHandler`. Outside an interactive run its UI never prompts and `notify` prints the message to stderr, colored as chalk would (`cli/project-trust.ts`).
- `ai.ConvertResponsesMessages`, `ai.ConvertResponsesTools` and their option structs are Pi's `openai-responses-shared` conversions as package-level functions; the Responses, Codex and Azure providers call the same code. The wire types are exported as `ResponsesInputItem`, `ResponsesTool` and `ResponsesToolFormat`.
- The in-process extension `ProviderOAuth.RefreshToken` callback receives the refresh's `context.Context`, as Pi's `refreshToken(credentials, signal)` receives its `AbortSignal`, so a cancelled refresh reaches the extension.
- `env.SshArgumentsWith(target, strictHostKeys, knownHostsFile)` is the exported three-parameter form of Pi's `sshArguments`.
- The settings selector's `MermaidRenderingMode` and `OutputPad` values are typed (`"off" | "final" | "streaming"`, `0 | 1`) in `SettingsConfig`, `SettingsCallbacks` and the settings manager.
- Summarization (compaction, branch summary, bug report) takes Pi's `retry` and `callbacks` as `ai.RetryPolicy` and `ai.RetryCallbacks`; the private `compaction.RetryPolicy`, `RetryCallbacks` and `RetryOptions` are removed.
- `SessionEntry` (`coding.SessionEntry`, `extension.SessionEntry`) is Pi's `SessionEntry` union, an interface whose members are the entry types of `session-manager.ts`: message, thinking level change, model change, usage, compaction, branch summary, custom, custom message, context edit, label and session info entries. Read an entry's `type`, `id`, `parentId` and `timestamp` with `entry.Base()` (they were the `Base` field), and its JSON with `entry.Raw()`. An entry keeps its JSON as it was read, so members this build does not know and the member order survive loading and rewriting a session file, as in Pi; a record that no member describes (a newer entry type, or a known type with ill-typed members) is a `RawEntry`. The session file's `FileEntry` is the entries and `SessionHeader`.
- Gemini, Mistral and pi-messages streams append each read to their buffer and search only the new bytes, so an event that arrives in many reads (a large tool input sent whole) costs memory in proportion to its size instead of its square (a 640 KB event in 1 KB reads: about 4 to 10 MB allocated instead of about 215 MB).
- Request bodies with escapes JSON.stringify does not write (an ESC in tool output, `=>` in code) are rewritten in spans between backslashes rather than a byte at a time (a 200 KB write body: about 50 µs instead of 190 to 300 µs).
- A finished tool call's arguments reach completions and Mistral requests without a copy, so the request after a 200 KB write call allocates about 3 to 7 KB instead of about 235 KB; completions function tools are written straight into the request encoder (about 1.1 KB per tool instead of 2.5 KB).
- The request string cache frees the encodings of history that three request builds in a row did not send (after a compaction, /new, /resume or a branch switch) instead of keeping them until its 64 MB limit; history every request sends stays cached.
- Completions, Responses and Codex WebSocket streams no longer republish the assistant message before each record when the record's changes were already published with its event (one allocation fewer per delta), and common Responses delta events are decoded without reflection (about 220 ns instead of 700 ns per event, no allocation). Event order and the message each consumer observes are unchanged.
- A faux provider stream paced by `tokensPerSecond` waits for each chunk on one timer that it re-arms, instead of creating a timer and two callbacks per chunk; an unpaced stream no longer allocates a callback per chunk either (13 to 10 allocations per paced chunk, 11 to 10 per unpaced one). The chunks and their timing are unchanged.
- Streaming response bodies are read without a goroutine round trip per read. A read whose bytes the HTTP connection already buffers completes on the caller (no goroutine wake, one allocation instead of four), and a read that waits for the network wakes one goroutine instead of three. Event order and timing seen by extensions and RPC clients are unchanged.
- OpenAI Chat Completions and Responses streams decode each record with fewer copies and no map decode: a record whose text holds an escape, as nearly every tool-argument delta of a file write does, is checked for a top-level `error` member by a scan instead of decoding all its members, and the SSE framing splits lines in place. A tool-argument delta goes from 19 allocations (1.9 KB) to 3 (0.5 KB). The events are unchanged.
- The SSE idle watchdog reads a streamed response about ten times faster: it finds line ends with a byte search instead of a per-byte loop that copied every byte, and looks for reasoning markers in one pass per data line instead of one search per marker (1 MB of a large write's stream: 12 ms to about 1 ms). It ends a stalled stream and spares a reasoning one exactly as before.
- A Codex (ChatGPT subscription) stream record that arrives over many network reads is framed in linear time: each read's text is appended to one buffer and only the new bytes are searched for the end of the record, instead of the whole buffer being copied and searched again on every read (a 640 KB record: 223 MB allocated before, 8.3 MB after). A record's bytes are copied once for its syntax check, decode and raw form instead of three times.
- An Anthropic stream event that arrives over many network reads, such as a large tool input a gateway sends whole, is buffered in linear time: each read is appended to the line buffer and only its bytes are searched, instead of the whole buffer being copied and searched again on every read (a 640 KB event: 216 MB allocated before, 3.9 MB after). Well-formed UTF-8 is decoded without a per-character loop, which also speeds up Gemini streams.
- Streaming hands execution between the provider and the agent loop with far fewer coroutine switches: an await whose body would be resumed next anyway goes on without a switch, running the queued reactions ahead of it itself, and a provider's awaits that nothing can observe are made in one row at its next observable step. In a `-p` text run the faux pipeline drops from 9.3 to 4 switches per streamed delta. Event order, ticks and output bytes are unchanged.
- A Session event that has nothing queued ahead of it now runs its listeners on the goroutine that emitted it, as Pi's synchronous `_emit` does, instead of being handed to the Session's event goroutine and waited for. Event order, the message state each listener and extension handler sees, and the JSON and RPC output are unchanged; a streamed delta no longer wakes a goroutine for Session delivery.
- `Session.DiscardEvents` declares that nothing reads `Events`: the Session sends nothing on it and `FlushEvents` returns once every earlier event has run. Print mode (text and JSON) uses it instead of draining `Events` on a goroutine of its own.
- A streamed assistant update makes one snapshot of the partial message instead of two, the agent records the streaming message without copying it, a listener-only update skips the stream-state refresh when no Session listener is subscribed, and tool-call arguments are carried as bytes instead of being copied twice per tool call.
- Streaming now hands execution between the provider, the stream forwarders and the agent loop by coroutine switch instead of waking a goroutine, and it no longer allocates per-event executor bookkeeping. On a 200 KB streamed write, process CPU drops about 20%. Goroutine wake-ups per streamed delta drop from 17.6 to 3.4 (the 3.4 left are Session hops). Event order and output bytes are unchanged.
- Extension dispatch costs fewer goroutine wake-ups, socket reads and full-text rescans. A host dispatch (state push plus event request) now takes 1 socket read and about 3 wake-ups, down from 6 reads and 9.1 wake-ups. Streamed message_update deltas now cost the delta instead of the text so far: at 5,000 deltas a delta costs 25 µs, down from 313 µs. The stdout terminal probe behind the extension theme palette runs once per stdout file instead of once per state push, which removes about 11% of host CPU on a ten-extension RPC turn. The Go and Rust SDKs no longer copy the whole tool-call argument text for each delta, and the MCP stdio and SSE line splitters search only bytes they have not searched yet. Wire bytes, event order and the values extensions receive are unchanged.
- Session lines and every other JSON.stringify-mode encode take one pass: the shared JSON codec gained a JSON.stringify mode (`MarshalStringify`/`AppendStringify`) that writes U+2028/U+2029 raw and rewrites nested Marshaler output while compacting it, replacing jsstring's whole-output `jsText` rewrite and its extra copy. A `StringifyAppender` type is spliced without being re-read, so a 200 KB tool call under four MarshalJSON levels encodes in about 0.1-0.2 ms instead of 3.7-5 ms and allocates once. String escaping scans eight bytes at a time (jsparity and the codec), `UnmarshalTrusted` skips the validity pass for JSON PiG wrote itself, `orderedjson.UnmarshalFields` decodes once instead of three times, `jsonstringify.IsCanonical` accepts objects of any size, and the encoder, jsstring and SafeJsonStringify pools drop buffers above 64 KiB. Output bytes are unchanged.
- Appending a message to a session encodes its line in one pass: content blocks, agent messages and the session's message entry append their JSON.stringify text in place instead of each level returning a copy that the next one compacts, the codec no longer doubles a buffer that already fits a long string, and the proven decoded form of a tool call takes its argument member order from the walk that writes the arguments instead of writing and re-parsing them. A message whose text exceeds 64 KiB is encoded into one buffer sized for it; a shorter line is encoded in the codec's pooled buffer and kept at its length, so the record the session keeps holds no spare room. A 1 MiB assistant turn now allocates 1.06 MB per append (1.01x its payload, 52 allocations) instead of 11.8 MB (118 allocations), and takes about 1-2 ms instead of 10-13 ms. Session line bytes are unchanged.
- Streaming an assistant response costs less per delta. The interactive chat block reads the message's block kinds, texts, stop reason and error text in place instead of copying the whole message and every tool-call argument on each delta: an update allocates 6 times whatever the arguments hold (525 times with a 256-argument tool call before). A host with several extensions prepares each message_update payload once per published partial and hands it to every subscribed extension, so per-delta host work no longer grows with the number of extensions: 19 µs per delta for three extensions, down from 55 µs, and 42 µs for ten, down from 200 µs. A message_start, message_update or message_end with no extension handler no longer builds the handler context. Frames, event order and the values extensions and the terminal receive are unchanged.
- Forking a session from a file (`--fork`) writes each entry once through a buffered writer instead of encoding it again, rewriting the escaped encoding on the slow normalization path and decoding it to check its type: about 4x fewer bytes allocated and one write per 64 KB instead of one per entry. The forked file's contents are unchanged.
- The settings manager's getters read the merged settings under its read lock instead of deep-copying all of the settings on every call (36 allocations per call to none for the scalar getters, which run per provider request, per assistant message end and per rendered tool card). A getter that returns a list, map or pointer still returns a copy; the values are unchanged.
- An extension state push no longer encodes, decodes and re-encodes the settings on every handler dispatch: the settings manager encodes the extension settings object once per settings change and the push carries those bytes (about 43 µs, 339 allocations and 20 KB per push to none while the settings are unchanged). `pi.getSettings()` returns the same object as before.
- Opening a session no longer builds the `/tree` search text of every message line. `/tree` reads the texts after its first screen is drawn, so a session that never opens `/tree` skips that work. On a 314 MB session, opening it uses about 15% less CPU, allocates 19% fewer bytes (27% fewer allocations) and keeps 11% less heap. `/tree` draws its first screen as fast as before and shows and finds the same rows. The first search after opening `/tree` takes about 10-18 ms longer.
- `pig -p` reads `auth.json` and `models-store.json` once per offline model operation instead of once per provider: a print run with four tool turns stats `auth.json` 15 times instead of 433 and `models-store.json` 3 times instead of 85. Each operation still sees writes by this process, and the next operation sees writes by other processes, as Pi 1.1.0 does.
- Credential reads no longer stat `auth.json` under the read-state mutex or take the model registry's write lock, so concurrent reads no longer queue (`BenchmarkAuthStorageReadHit` at 8 CPUs: 1,612 to 363 ns/op).
- Offline catalog restores and availability passes without extension providers run their providers on the calling goroutine, in provider order, and a catalog restore no longer starts a waiter and a publication goroutine per provider: the same print run starts 107 goroutines instead of 535.
- An idle interactive session no longer wakes the spinner ticker every 80 ms, and after a run plus 500 ms of idle it returns freed heap to the OS once when the heap grew.
- Commands that print a lot of output use less CPU and memory: the bash tool reads its pipe into one reused buffer, decodes and truncates its output without copying it, and `!` commands no longer redraw the whole screen for every chunk of output (they draw at most once per frame, as Pi does). 10 MiB of command output costs pig about half the CPU through the bash tool and a third through `!`.
- When `GOMAXPROCS` is not set in the environment, PiG runs on at most 2 Go processors (fewer when the runtime default is lower), so a streamed turn no longer wakes idle processors at each hand-off. Measured on a 32-core host: `-p` CPU is about 18% lower, a 200 KB write about 13%, an interactive session about 16%, and idle CPU about 45%. Work you wait on still runs at the full default width: opening a session, the `/resume` picker's listing, `/tree`, export, fork, compaction and extension startup. A `GOMAXPROCS` or `GOGC` you set is left as you set it. Output is unchanged.
- Tool calls that run in parallel now use every processor the Go runtime would use by default, instead of the 2-processor cap PiG applies when `GOMAXPROCS` is not set. In-process tools like `ls` and `find` no longer slow down when the model calls several at once: a turn with 2 `ls` and 2 `find` calls took 74.2 ms under the cap against 67.1 ms uncapped. A single tool call and sequential tool execution stay capped. Output and tool event order are unchanged.
- A streamed tool call's partial arguments are parsed only when something reads them. The executor-managed stream builder (OpenAI Completions and Responses, Google, Mistral, Bedrock) keeps each delta's argument text instead of parsing it, and a reader of the published message (an event listener, the TUI, an extension, `StreamingMessage`) parses it when it reads, resuming where its last read stopped. Final arguments are parsed once at the end of the call, as before. In `-p` text mode, a 16-byte argument delta on OpenAI Completions allocates 21.7 objects instead of 71.5.
- A `message_update` that nothing can observe is no longer built or delivered. In `-p` text mode (the Session discards its events, has no listener, no extension `message_update` handler and no termination hold) the Agent skips the update's partial-message copy, its listeners and the reactions it awaits, and only records the partial for `Agent.StreamingMessage`, which builds the same message when asked. Every mode that shows or forwards updates (JSON, RPC, the TUI, an extension handler, any Agent or Session listener) gets them as before, and an observer that appears during a stream gets every later update. A streamed 16-byte argument delta in `-p` text mode allocates 13.5 objects instead of 45 on the faux provider.
- A finished tool call's arguments are encoded once: later requests, session lines and events reuse the bytes while the arguments are unchanged, so a 200 KB write call no longer costs a pass over its text on every later turn (the large-write follow-up request builds in about 55 µs instead of about 200 µs on completions, and 10 µs instead of 1.3 ms on Responses). A change to the arguments, at any depth, is still sent.
- The request string cache finds a string the previous request already sent by the string itself, without hashing and comparing its text, so a long session's request no longer reads its whole history again (75-prompt long-session completions request: about 1.6 ms instead of 3 to 8 ms).
- Anthropic content blocks and tools, Responses tools and the Responses body are written straight into the request encoder instead of being encoded apart and copied in.
- A cached Codex WebSocket turn compares the request with the previous one as JSON texts, as Pi does, instead of decoding the whole body into maps, cloning it and comparing the trees (2,000 input items: about 7 ms and 2,100 allocations instead of about 130 ms and 260,000), and keeps the previous request as its text rather than a decoded copy.
- The markdown renderer's pooled lexer state no longer keeps the strings of the last rendered document while idle. A state whose buffers grew past 64 KiB on a long paragraph is dropped instead of being pooled. Before, one 1.1 MB paragraph left 65 MB of code points and tokens pinned in the pool. Rendered output is unchanged.
- The faux provider's usage estimate remembers a session's previous prompt as its messages and the length of each one's text, not as two copies of the serialized prompt. A model call writes out only the messages it added or changed. Over a 75-prompt session of about 4.2M tokens (17.2 MB of history), the provider used to keep 39.6 MB alive after the last call (2.3x the history) and now keeps 0.13 MB. One estimate over a history of 40 KiB reads takes 5.2 µs, down from 458 µs. The usage numbers are unchanged.
- OpenAI-compatible chat completion streams decode each SSE record with at most two allocations, down from 7 for a text delta and 13 for an escaped tool-call delta. The decoder decodes the chunk while it checks the record, which replaces a separate JSON validation pass. Chunk arrays are reused from record to record, and escaped strings are copied once. Records, events and their order are unchanged.
- Finding the end of each record no longer searches the rest of a socket read for a carriage return, so a read that holds many records costs time in proportion to their number instead of its square. 4,096 records in one read went from 74 ms to 5 ms.
- Extension diagnostics that pass their own terminal capabilities no longer run the tmux hyperlink probe (`tmux display-message`, up to 250 ms) or fill the capability cache: `GetTerminalColorMode` detects capabilities only when called without them, as upstream's default parameter does.
- The models.json schema is built at the first models.json validation instead of when every `pig` process starts (34 KB in 290 allocations of package initialization).
- The Mermaid block parser is built at the first Markdown text that reaches the Mermaid transform instead of at process start (15 KB of package initialization).
- The PiG identity values (D26) are Go constants that a test holds to `identity.json`, so a process no longer decodes `identity.json` at start (16 KB in 105 allocations).
- An interactive start parses the bundled changelog only when the recorded changelog version differs from the running one; the first start and every start of the same version skip it. What's New, the recorded version and the install ping are unchanged.
- The footer's 250 ms reftable poll (and the 1 s WSL HEAD poll) issues one `stat` per poll on Unix systems, instead of `os.Stat` plus a reflection and `fmt` read of the change time: 1 allocation per poll instead of 4. The cadence and the change detection (modification time, change time, size) are unchanged.
- JSON string encoding in the Durable core (`jv.AppendString`, which the history encoder and the session records use, and which `jsparity.AppendString` now is) finds the runs that need no escape eight bytes at a time and copies them whole: a 200 KB source text encodes in about 26 µs instead of 127 µs. The encoded bytes are unchanged; the TinyGo core still builds (its module grows by 458 bytes).
- A Go or Rust extension build reads and hashes the staged extension SDK once per process: later builds, and the SDK fingerprint a build records, check each SDK source file's size and modification time instead of reading the SDK again (about 3.5 ms instead of 18 ms for the Go SDK's 206 sources, and 145 KB instead of 1.6 MB allocated). An SDK changed in place or restaged is hashed again. The cache key folds in the SDK's digest rather than its files, so Go and Rust extensions cached by an earlier PiG build once more.
- The Durable edit tool builds its display diff and its unified patch from one windowed line diff, reading only the lines the views show, as the coding agent's edit tool does: a one-line edit in a 230 KB file allocates 7 KB instead of 586 KB (4,028 to 79 allocations) and runs about 4 times faster. The diff and the patch are unchanged.
- The edit tool's display diff and patch come from one line diff that skips the unchanged lines before and after the change (Pi-Bolt bf325340): a one-line edit in a 230 KB file takes 0.15-0.17 ms for both views instead of 1.6-2.1 ms, and allocates 7 KB instead of 2 MB. The diff, patch and first changed line are unchanged.
- Reading a window of a large file opens it once (access, image detection and read share one descriptor) and decodes only the selected lines: 20 lines of a 5.8 MB file allocate at most the file plus 64 KB instead of 13 MB.
- Write and edit reserve their place in the file mutation queue from the path alone, edit arguments already in the `{path, edits: [...]}` shape are not re-parsed, and files are written without copying the content (about 0.5-2.5 MB less per 200 KB call).
- `ls` lowercases and weighs each name once when sorting (a 3000-entry directory: 167k to under 12k allocations); grep's context window no longer clones its lines per match.
- Tool-call admission no longer re-encodes arguments that validation left unchanged and that are already in JSON.stringify form, and each built-in tool's argument schema is encoded once instead of on every call. On a 200 KB write call, admission allocates 604 KB in 91 allocations instead of 1.76 MB in 213.
- When nothing can see `tool_execution_update` (print text mode without extension update handlers), bash and PowerShell run no update scheduler. Every mode that shows or forwards updates gets them as before, and so does an observer that appears while the command runs.
- `BashOperationsExecOptions.OnData` documents that the slice is only valid during the call.
- Interactive mode schedules throttled frames on its own loop: a frame no longer starts a timer goroutine that hands the render back to the loop, as Pi's requestRender arms one setTimeout on its event loop. Streamed answers paint the same frames at the same 16 ms throttle with no goroutine per frame.
- A keystroke reaches the editor in one goroutine hand-off instead of about four, and paints about twice as fast (about 57 µs to 27 µs from the terminal pipe to the paint on a Linux host): the terminal reader frames its reads and routes each key to the interactive loop itself, lets the loop run before it waits for the next read, and no longer waits for the loop's answer to the last key of a read. Input order, the hold while an extension's input listener decides, and one paint per terminal read are unchanged.
- The interactive TUI no longer reads the working directory for every tool path it links (`PathToFileURL` resolves an absolute path without `process.cwd()`, as Node does), and no longer reads the terminal size for every frame: while the SIGWINCH handler runs, the size is kept between resizes, as Node keeps `process.stdout.columns` and `rows`. Windows still reads the console size every time.
- Fullscreen frames write their rows without `fmt` and start the frame buffer at the last frame's length (47 to 37 allocations per streaming frame). The assistant message block keeps its children across streamed deltas: a tool-call argument delta allocates nothing (was 6) and a text delta 5 times (was 11). The bytes sent to the terminal are unchanged.
- Tool card renderers no longer reread the whole tool call or result on every update or frame. The edit renderers decode their arguments only when the bytes change: 3.7 KB per update for 256 KB of edits, down from 3.7 MB. The edit preview box keeps its diff rows per width, so a redraw of a 256 KB diff allocates nothing instead of 18 MB. Shell result components are built once per update and keep their rows per width: 0 B per frame for 256 KB of output, down from 4.4 MB. Persisted shell details are read without a JSON round trip.
- Clean tool output and `!` bash chunks are no longer copied twice to strip ANSI sequences; a 256 KB clean result saves 512 KB per card update.
- `mcp.Transport.Send` and `mcp.OrderedSender.SendOrdered` take the send signal as their leading `context.Context` (`Send(ctx, message)`, `SendOrdered(ctx, message, placed)`) instead of a `TransportSendOptions` with a `Signal` field, which is removed. As in Pi, the HTTP transport does not dispatch a message once its context has ended (it fails with `McpAbortError`), and the stdio and in-memory transports ignore it.
- The interactive mode returns memory to the system 500 ms after a run ends instead of 2 s, also after a run shorter than one spinner frame, and typing during the wait restarts it instead of cancelling it. On a 75-prompt session the memory 2 s after the last answer drops from about 131 MB to about 90 MB.
- A long interactive transcript keeps about 1.4 KB of render state per row instead of 2.1 KB: containers no longer keep spare line slots, and a finished answer drops the caches it used while streaming.
- Extension SDKs: a provider object's `OAuthAuth.Login` takes the app's login options as its second argument. In Go it is `func(AuthInteraction, LoginOptions) (*Credential, error)`, and `sdk.LoginOptions.GetDeviceID` is nil when the app supplies no device ID. In Python it is `login(interaction, options)` with `pig_sdk.LoginOptions(get_device_id, agent_name)`. In Rust it is `Fn(AuthInteraction, LoginOptions)`. A login that ignores the options takes and ignores the argument. Calling another extension's provider object passes your options on to it. `pig extension upgrade` reports the Go change with its remedy.
- A streamed OpenAI-compatible chat-completion chunk that holds a plain text delta is decoded without reflection, which lowers the CPU of every streamed token; any other chunk (tool calls, usage, errors, unknown or case-folded member names, malformed JSON) is decoded exactly as before.
- A streamed Chat Completions record that the chunk decode reads is no longer scanned again, for a provider stream event observer with nothing to do, by the overflow check: the decode reports whether the record parses as a value. A record the decode declines is checked exactly as before.
- `make parity-live`, the parity step of `make release-check`, runs every scenario under the scheduler's default concurrency groups with a 90-minute timeout measured from a full run. It used to run them one at a time under a 30-minute limit and could not finish. Scenarios that make real model calls with the owner's credentials carry the `owner-credentials` tag: `make parity-live` names them as not run there, and `make parity-live-owner` (with `PIG_PARITY_REAL_AUTH=<auth.json>`) runs them on the owner's machine. The scenario lint requires the tag on every `real-llm` scenario.
- The parity runner builds a `cli-mode` scenario's Go probes before its timed runs. A `go run ./PACKAGE` pig side starts the built binary, and pig scripts run Go tests through `test/parity/testdata/prebuilt-go-test.sh` from test binaries built for the packages the scenario lists in `[env] pig_go_tests`. A run's runtime and timeout no longer include compiling and linking, which on a loaded host with a cold build cache outlasted the 60 s pig timeout of `20-session-selector-search`.
- An extension's `turn_end` and `agent_before_settle` handlers cost less in a long session: the host no longer copies every message of the branch for each context preview, and encodes only the messages that are new since the previous boundary instead of the whole history. A handler written in Go still receives its own copies.
- `before_agent_start` and `tool_call` handlers in extension processes cost less per prompt: the host encodes the event once for every handler that runs after no edit, and skips applying a handler's reply that repeats the one applied last.
- Extensions fused into a Piglet Binary receive events with less work: the host hands them each event's JSON by reference instead of through an in-memory socket, and each event payload is decoded once for every handler instead of once per handler. Every handler still receives its own copy of the event.
- The host writes an event's payload to an extension without a second pass over its bytes, and a turn's session context is no longer re-encoded for every handler. The bytes an extension receives are unchanged.
- Extension boundary events cost less per turn: the context preview no longer decodes every session entry for each handler, and does not decode it at all when every handler runs in an extension process.
- A prompt turn allocates about a third less: tool declarations are encoded into one buffer for the token estimates, and the built-in tools decode their parameter schemas once instead of every turn.
- Streaming Markdown with emphasis allocates less per token: the one-pass span renderer shares its constant code tables.
- `pig piglet build` and `pig piglet verify` hash a Piglet Binary and each release asset by streaming the file. Before, each digest read the whole file (70 MB and more) into memory first. The digests are unchanged.
- `pig piglet build` hashes the Pig source tree and each local Package tree through one reused buffer. Before, the source digest read every file into memory (484 MB allocated for the 15,800-file PiG tree) and each Package tree digest allocated a 32 KiB copy buffer per file. The digests are unchanged.
- A request for a stripped built-in names the Piglet and how to keep it: `The TypeScript and JavaScript extension runtime is stripped from the Piglet "a2a" (strip.features: node-extensions); to keep it, remove node-extensions from strip.features in the Piglet manifest and rebuild`. A strip is opt-in, so the author who wrote the entry can remove it. Without a Piglet name (stock PiG, tests) the message is unchanged.
- A Piglet Binary that strips `extension-sdk-go` no longer links the Go type checker: `pig extension upgrade` analyzes an extension with `go/types`, and that code stayed in a Binary that cannot build Go extensions. In an all-strip build the `go/*` code shrinks from 965 KB to 272 KB of symbols (the Binary from 56.1 to 53.7 MB before `-s -w`); `pig extension upgrade` in such a Binary reports that the Go SDK is compiled out.
- PiG now targets Pi main at 3daa2028 (after Pi 1.2.0): `pig --version` reports the upstream version `1.2.0+3daa2028`, and the model catalogs and vendored Pi runtime follow that commit.
- Codemode stops a script that only chains promise jobs, such as `while (true) await null`, at the next interrupt poll. The VM drains the promise job queue one job at a time and polls the interrupt after every 50 jobs, as Pi 1.2.0 does ([748b351f](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/commit/748b351f)). A job that fails now reports `Error: Promise job failed` instead of quickjs-wasi's `Job execution error` text.
- Port Pi 1.2.0's decoupling of MCP from codemode (24382bed, c661bc57) in `codemode` and `durable/codemode`: `McpTypescriptPreamble` and `McpStructuredContentSchema` are gone, `RenderToolOutputType` and the declarations built on it render every output schema as its own type (an MCP `CallToolResult` schema is a plain type), the durable `codemode` section no longer lists "Shared MCP Types", and `image()` errors describe the accepted image block as `{ type: "image", data, mimeType }` instead of an MCP image block.
- The `codemode` description no longer includes "Shared MCP Types". It describes a result object two levels deep (`{ content, structuredContent?: { hits }, isError? }`) and says a tool whose declaration returns an object also resolves with it on failure (Pi c661bc57, 42705224).
- Extensions of every runtime (Node, Go, Python, Rust SDKs) can define `reachesTools` on a tool and `aliases` on a tool namespace. The host asks the extension with `tool_reaches_tools`.
- `codemode.SchemaToType` and the declarations built on it show a property's constraints that the TypeScript type cannot express as tags on its comment line, as Pi 1.2.0 does (8a9c051f) ([#10707](https://github.com/earendil-works/pi/issues/10707)): `// @integer @minimum 0 @maximum 30000 @default 0`. The tags cover `integer`, numeric bounds, `multipleOf`, string length, `pattern`, `format`, array length, `uniqueItems`, a non-null `default` and `deprecated`. A nullable `anyOf`/`oneOf` field takes its constraints from the non-null branch, draft-04 boolean `exclusiveMinimum`/`exclusiveMaximum` show as exclusive bounds, and property descriptions also split on U+2028 and U+2029. A type over `MaxChars` is rendered again without the tags before it becomes `unknown`.
- A nested call to a tool with a `StructuredOutputSchema` whose error result has no structured output now gives the caller the error's text as an error `tool_error` diagnostic, as Pi 1.2.0 does (8bb180be), instead of as the structured output (which is only ever the tool's own value): one diagnostic, the text items joined with newlines, and none when the result already has an error diagnostic or structured output. The model's call keeps the text in its output. Tools without a schema are unchanged: callers get their output.
- The Go `env` package mirrors the terminal API of Pi's `terminal.ts` under its names: `RemoteTerminal.Exited` (an exit that can be awaited any number of times), `NewRemoteTerminal`, and `Write` of text or bytes. A write of more than one frame reaches the terminal whole and in order.
- The upstream Pi target is one setting, `.upstream/pin`: a release tag or a pinned pi-mono commit (displayed as `1.1.0+42a3497d`). `PI_UPSTREAM_REF=<tag|commit>` overrides it for one run, and `make pin-bump PI_UPSTREAM_REF=<tag|commit>` moves it and regenerates every file derived from it. A commit pin builds its Pi oracle from source. See `docs/parity/upstream-pin.md`.
- `pig mcp list --json` reports `resources: true` for servers that offer resources instead of `resources` and `resourceTemplates` counts, and `/mcp` and `pig mcp list` say `resources` / `offers resources` instead of counting them. PiG no longer lists resources when connecting, as Pi f4cca2dc now does.
- `pig mcp login --timeout` now limits the whole sign-in, including requests to the authorization server, instead of only the wait for the browser.
- PiG now targets Pi 1.2.0 (was 1.1.0): the upstream pin, the vendored Pi runtime and the parity ledgers are rebased on the 1.2.0 release.
- Changed `outputPad` to also apply to `!` command output, tool output, and branch, compaction and skill summary blocks, and to extension custom messages and entries. Changing it in `/settings` now updates the transcript in place instead of rebuilding it.
- The release notes draft (`make release-notes-draft`) thanks the co-authors that `Co-authored-by:` trailers credit, as well as commit authors. A person credited under two names or two emails is thanked once, and the maintainer, bots and coding agents are not listed.
- The Go SDK page now gives the full `coding.DefaultAgentDir()` order (`PIG_CODING_AGENT_DIR`, then the configuration root `PIG_HOME`, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/pig` or `~/.pig`, or Pi's directories with `PIG_USE_PI_DIRS=1`) and cites divergence D2.
- The Windows page now says that Pi keeps its configuration in `~/.pi/agent` (or `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR`) and reads neither `PIG_HOME` nor `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, and cites divergence D2 for PiG's configuration root.
- The subprocess wire type `CommandDecl` no longer has the `Args` field. The host never read it, and Pi's `registerCommand` has no such option.
- `oauth.McpOAuthState.TokensExpireAt` is a `*float64`, as Pi's `tokensExpireAt` is a JavaScript number. A fractional or very large `expires_in` is kept as Pi keeps it, and a state file that Pi wrote with a fractional value loads. When `expires_in` overflows the expiry to infinity, the state is saved with `tokensExpireAt: null`, as `JSON.stringify` writes it, and the token reads back as expired.
- Opening or resuming a long session reads its file on several cores: each line is validated, probed and given its `/tree` search text on a worker, and the index takes the lines in file order, so it holds what a one-core load holds. On a 313 MB session with 50,722 entries, loading the file takes 0.45 s instead of 2.7 s at `GOMAXPROCS=8` (Linux). With one core the load is unchanged.
- `mcpext.Extension.ToolCall` takes the call's input as `json.RawMessage` (the event's `InputJSON()`), not `map[string]any`.
- `settings.json` top-level `commandPrefix`, `showImages`, `imageWidthCells`, `clearOnShrink`, `imageAutoResize` and `blockImages` no longer configure anything, as in Pi, which has no such keys. Use `shellCommandPrefix`, `terminal.showImages`, `terminal.imageWidthCells`, `terminal.clearOnShrink`, `images.autoResize` and `images.blockImages`. The flat keys stay in the file as written.
- The model objects sent to extensions no longer carry PiG's extra members `modelId`, `displayName`, `maxOutputTokens`, `inputCostPer1M`, `outputCostPer1M`, `cacheReadCostPer1M` and `cacheWriteCostPer1M`, which Pi's model objects do not have. Use `id`, `name`, `maxTokens` and `cost`. The Go and Rust SDKs' typed model views read the Pi members and are unaffected.
- The chord host manager keeps a child's reported activity counts as numbers: `ControlMessage`, `ChildStatus` and `Liveness` counts are `float64`, so a fractional count keeps the child busy as it does in Pi instead of being truncated to 0.
- `durable.NestedCallOptions.ArgumentsJSON` carries the arguments' JSON text in the caller's member order; `harness.NestedToolSlot.Arguments` is a `durable.JsonValue`.
- A program that runs the extension host without linking `coding/extension/pigsdk` now names that import when a Go or Rust extension build needs the staged SDK. Before, it said `this pig embeds no go extension SDK`. Programs that link package `coding`, including `pig`, already link it.
- The Rust SDK manifest now names the repository, homepage, readme, keywords and category, as the Python manifest does, so `cargo package` no longer warns and the crate page links back to PiG.
- **Breaking (Go SDK):** in a `before_agent_start` handler, `data["systemPrompt"]` is an `*sdk.SystemPrompt` instead of a string, because a Go map cannot run a getter. A handler that still reads a string panics (`data["systemPrompt"].(string)`) or reads an empty prompt (the `, ok` form). Read it with `Get()`, which returns `(string, error)`; the value encodes to JSON as the current prompt string. Migration: `data["systemPrompt"].(string)` becomes `data["systemPrompt"].(*sdk.SystemPrompt).Get()`.
- Rust SDK: `Context::current_system_prompt(data)` reads Pi's `event.systemPrompt` and `ctx.getSystemPrompt()` in a `before_agent_start` handler, because the handler holds `data` mutably and `ctx` cannot read it.
- Opening or resuming a session file checks each line's JSON with a single-pass validator (`internal/jsonvalid`) instead of encoding/json's byte-at-a-time scanner, about three times faster on session-shaped lines (1.07 GB/s against 0.33 GB/s). It accepts and rejects exactly the same lines, so the lines `JSON.parse` would throw on are still skipped; a differential against the scanner and Node's `JSON.parse` over every short text, random token texts, mutated session lines and the 10,000-level nesting limit backs this.
- A session no longer keeps a JSON copy of each message it holds beside the message itself, including a session started with `--no-session`, which never writes one. In Pi-Bolt's 75-prompt long workload (about 4.2M tokens of context), memory at the end drops from 130 MB to 73 MB. Every read of the session (RPC, extensions, export, `/tree`) returns the same bytes as before.
- A resumed session no longer keeps a JSON copy of each context message it reads back from the session file, beside the message itself, when the message writes the same bytes again. A resumed context retains about half as much memory per byte of context, and every read of the session returns the file's bytes as before.
- Startup with a `models.json` allocates about 4,500 fewer objects in print, JSON and RPC mode and about 10,000 fewer interactively: the models.json schema is built once per process, and its provider, model and override schemas share one copy of each Pi schema constant, as Pi does. Validation errors are unchanged.
- A new session file is written through a buffer the size of its records instead of a fixed 64 KB one, so the first message of a run allocates about 64 KB less. Session files and JSONL exports are unchanged.
- A TypeScript extension now receives one model registry snapshot when PiG binds the session's model operations at startup, instead of three. Each snapshot carries the whole model catalog, so interactive start with a TypeScript extension reaches `session_start` about 40 ms sooner on Linux. The final snapshot is unchanged.
- The first TypeScript extension start in a new PiG home links its copy of the Node runtime on several threads, about a third faster.
- PiG encodes the model registry snapshot it sends to a TypeScript extension about 30% faster, with 70% fewer allocations. The snapshot bytes are unchanged.
- In interactive mode, a TypeScript extension's runtime starts loading the full set of syntax-highlighting grammars after the extension has handled the startup `session_start` and `resources_discover` events, as Pi does. Previously the load ran first and delayed those handlers by about 50 ms.
- PiG starts with less work before `main`: the bundled model catalog builds each distinct thinking-level and header map once instead of once per model (828 maps down to 62), which cuts the catalog's package initialization from about 0.9 ms to 0.6 ms and 200 KB of allocations on every start, `pig --version` included.
- A print run no longer indexes the whole model catalog to look up its one startup model, and selecting a built-in theme no longer also loads the other built-in theme. A warm print run allocates about 8% less.
- A print or JSON run that calls no tool and starts no extension no longer writes the documentation bundle or the extension SDKs under `~/.pig`, which cost about 20 ms of CPU on a first run and 3 ms on every later one. PiG writes the docs bundle before anything other than PiG can read it (the first tool call, an extension start, interactive and RPC start, `pig docs path`) and stages the SDKs before the first extension starts. The written files are unchanged. `pig diagnose` now says unstaged SDKs stage when an extension loads.
- Resolving `--model` or `--provider` at startup no longer builds every bundled catalog model; it builds only the model it selects. With an explicit `--model`, startup also no longer lists the catalog a second time for the saved-default and session-restore steps it skips. Together this removes about 13,000 allocations and 2.5 MB from every print run. The selected model is unchanged.
- Process start does less work. The model catalog's `*bool` and `*string` members point at package-level values the linker lays out, so `ai`'s initialisation builds no pointers (365 KB / 7,799 allocations / 1.6 ms to 324 KB / 2,155 allocations / 1.0 ms). The call-id patterns of the request validator and the version and constraint patterns of the semantic-version package compile on first use; the semantic-version package is a vendored fork (`third_party/semver`, notice in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`). All package initialisers in the binary allocate 12,900 times instead of 20,300.
- Streamed responses allocate about half as many objects per delta between the provider and the session event stream, which lowers the CPU and GC cost of long answers. Event order and output are unchanged.
- On Linux, streamed provider responses are read without waking the Go runtime's monitor thread on every chunk, which removes several context switches per streamed delta. Output and error messages are unchanged.
- Anthropic Messages streams decode each well-formed chunk of response text in one step instead of character by character. Output is unchanged.
- A streamed model response that can be canceled registers its cancellation once per response instead of once per streamed event. Behavior is unchanged.
- A streamed OpenAI-compatible tool-call chunk (the function name and the arguments in pieces, as a model sends a file it writes) is decoded without reflection, and a published copy of an assistant message shares its text and thinking blocks instead of copying them, which lowers the CPU of a long streamed tool call. Chunks the fast decode does not understand are decoded exactly as before.
- Startup costs much less the deeper the working directory is. Each directory between it and the root is probed the way Pi's `existsSync` probes it, and the working directory's symlinks are resolved without building a new path at each component: a first prompt allocates about 22 objects per directory level instead of about 210.
- Skill discovery no longer resolves the symlinks of each missing `.agents/skills` directory above the working directory, so startup allocates less, and less the deeper the working directory is: a first prompt allocates about 55 KB less from a directory below `/tmp` and about 63 KB less two levels deeper.
- The `pig` binary links one YAML library instead of two copies of go-yaml under different import paths, about 285 KB smaller on every release platform.
- TypeScript and JavaScript extensions packed into one Node process cost about 4 MiB and 5 ms each instead of about 32 MiB and 27 ms. The process runs one IO worker for all of its extensions, not one each, and reads, validates and decodes each model catalog update the host sends to every extension once; each extension still gets its own copy of the models it reads. With 10 extensions the process peaks at about 140 MiB instead of 385 MiB, and `-p` starts in about 259 ms instead of 464 ms.
- A signed Piglet Binary no longer hashes its whole executable at every start. After a passing start it records the check in `state/piglet-verify/verified.json` under the PiG config root, keyed by the Binary's resolved path, file identity (device, inode, size, modification and status-change times; on Windows the volume serial number, file index, size, last write time and change time), signature block and trust policy. A later start with every field unchanged still checks the signature, signer and manifest but skips the hash: on an Apple M4 Pro a 63 MB Binary's `-p` start drops from 56.1 ms to 33.3 ms, against 31.1 ms for Stock PiG. Any change to the file or the trust policy, or a missing or damaged cache, causes a full check.
